1	# Task
2	
3	You will oversee 3 separate trading processes.
4	
5	- **The taker** (`taker/`) — the firm's existing Python strategy. Legacy code;
6	  you own it now. It runs alongside your work.
7	- **The quoter** (`strategy/`) — you build this.
8	- **The hedger** (`hedger/`) — you build this.
9	
10	Your two jobs:
11	
12	## Job 1 — a quoting strategy
13	
14	Write a **low-risk, profitable quoting strategy**: it should make its money
15	providing two-sided liquidity, not taking directional bets.
16	
17	- **Language:** any language supported by NATS, **except Python**.
18	
19	## Job 2 — keep the desk flat (the hedger)
20	
21	The desk's **combined position** — taker + quoter + hedger together — should
22	be kept low. Build a hedger process that makes that true:
23	when the desk's other seats accumulate exposure, the hedger reduces it,
24	promptly, and keeps it low.
25	
26	- How the desk's processes coordinate is up to you — you already have a
27	  message bus.
28	- **Language:** your choice (Python is allowed here).
29	
30	The main purpose of the hedger is risk control. Having a small open position for a long time
31	is relatively low risk. Having a very large open position even for just a second is high risk
32	(a large open position combined with a large market move that goes in the wrong direction could
33	put you out of business).
34	
35	## Mechanics
36	
37	- Each seat ships as a **Docker container**: `taker/` (provided), `strategy/`
38	  and `hedger/` (yours — put code and a `Dockerfile` in each).
39	  `./run.sh --sim --strategy` brings up the whole stack: exchange + sample
40	  market + all three seats (equivalently
41	  `docker compose --profile sim --profile strategy up`).
42	- Each seat sends orders with its own sender id, which it reads from the environment.
43	  Our grading script injects real values; fall back to sensible defaults for local dev.
44	  - **`$SENDER`** (quoter),
45	  - **`$TAKER_SENDER`** (taker),
46	  - **`$HEDGER_SENDER`** (hedger).
47	
48	- Every seat connects to the exchange at **`$NATS_URL`**
49	  (`nats://nats:4222` inside the compose network) — no host dependencies.
50	- Grading builds and runs your Dockerfiles on **`linux/amd64`** — build
51	  from source inside the Dockerfile; don't `COPY` in binaries compiled on
52	  your machine.
53	- See `README.md` to run everything and `PROTOCOL.md` for the wire protocol.
54	
55	### How it's graded
56	
57	Your desk runs unattended in our private grading market simulation for a
58	fixed session. When the session ends, resting orders are cancelled and any
59	remaining position is liquidated against the book.
60	We look at some risk and profitability metrics, as well as code quality, design quality,
61	and how you direct your AI agent. The grading market is not the sample market.
62	
63	We expect you to use an AI agent; the question is not whether you used one but how.
64	Two things matter to us, roughly equally:
65	- a desk that works — quoting profitably while keeping the position flat is a real result, not a formality
66	- and a clear account of how you got there.
67	Treat this as a realistic codebase: the documentation is incomplete in places, and the code you have been
68	given is now yours, whatever state it is in. NOTES.md and TRANSCRIPT.txt are where you show us the
69	reasoning behind the result — what you assumed, what you checked, what surprised you. We would rather see
70	how you convinced yourself something was true than a tidy write-up composed afterwards; if you built
71	something along the way to answer a question, include it. Be ready to explain any line you ship.
72	
73	## Deliverables
74	
75	- **`strategy/` and `hedger/`** with working Dockerfiles — plus whatever
76	  changes you made anywhere else in the repo, at the same relative paths
77	  (e.g. if you changed `taker/`, include your `taker/`). From a clean
78	  checkout, `./run.sh --sim --strategy` must bring up your working desk.
79	- **`NOTES.md`** — a short notes file: your design decisions, including any
80	  assumptions you made and hurdles you had to tackle.
81	- **`TRANSCRIPT.txt`** — your complete AI agent transcript. A raw export or
82	  a copy-paste is fine; if you used multiple sessions, concatenate them
83	  into this one file.
84	- Include any probes, helper scripts or test tooling you wrote — we want to
85	  see them.
86

88	
89	Submit a single file, a compressed directory, `<your_email>.tar.gz` or

1	# Trading-desk task
2	
3	## Notes
4	
5	Use an AI agent of your choice to help you solve this task.
6	
7	Your task, and supporting docs, are deliberately left incomplete and ambiguous,
8	to test your ability to work in a realistic subpar codebase.
9	
10	
11	## Brief:
12	
13	You trade for **Cannon Capital**, a startup prop trading firm.
14	
15	You have been assigned to a desk that trades a single exchange with 100,000 usd margin.
16	Your job is to expand the desks suite of trading processes.
17	Contracts on this exchange are cash-settled and worth exactly their listed price in usd.
18	
19	Read **`TASK.md`** for the full brief and **`PROTOCOL.md`** for the exchange
20	wire protocol. Below is some info on getting started.
21	
22	
23	## Prerequisites
24	
25	You are given a simulated exchange (a Docker image) that speaks a simple
26	message protocol over [NATS](https://nats.io).
27	
28	You need:
29	
30	- Docker + Docker Compose.
31	- An x86-64 machine, or emulation: the provided exchange image is
32	  `linux/amd64`. On Apple Silicon, Docker Desktop emulates it automatically
33	  (a platform warning is normal). On an arm64 Linux box, enable binfmt
34	  first: `docker run --privileged --rm tonistiigi/binfmt --install amd64`.
35	  Everything you build yourself runs natively either way.
36	- Python 3 if you want to run the taker on the host while probing
37	  (`taker/`, see its README) — in the full stack it runs as a container.
38	- The `nats` CLI is handy for probing (optional):
39	  https://github.com/nats-io/natscli — `nats context` defaults to
40	  `nats://localhost:4222`, which is what the exchange exposes.
41	
42	## Run the exchange
43	
44	```bash
45	./run.sh            # NATS + exchange
46	./run.sh --sim      # NATS + exchange + a sample market to trade against
47	```
48	
49	(Equivalently: `docker compose up` / `docker compose --profile sim up`.) The
50	first run loads the exchange image; NATS is published on
51	**`nats://localhost:4222`** for development (handy with the `nats` CLI).
52	
53	- **Without `--sim`** the book starts empty — good for controlled probing (you
54	  place orders and see exactly what happens).
55	- **With `--sim`** a simplistic sample market runs: background liquidity,
56	  prices that move around, casual takers.
57	
58	Stop with Ctrl-C; `docker compose down` to clean up.
59	
60	## Run your desk
61	
62	The three trading processes will live in **`taker/`** (provided), **`strategy/`** and
63	**`hedger/`**. Once your code + Dockerfiles are in
64	place:
65	
66	```bash
67	docker compose --profile sim --profile strategy up   # exchange + sample market + all three seats
68	```
69	
70	(`./run.sh --sim --strategy` is the same thing — exactly what grading runs.)
71	
72	Inside the compose network every seat reaches the exchange at
73	`nats://nats:4222`. While developing you can also run any seat directly on
74	the host against `nats://localhost:4222` — but the final submission must work
75	as containers from a clean checkout.

1	# Exchange protocol v2.5
2	
3	The exchange speaks **space-separated ASCII messages over NATS**. Fields never
4	contain spaces. There are three things to talk to:
5	
6	| What                 | NATS subject              | Mechanism            |
7	|----------------------|---------------------------|----------------------|
8	| Order entry          | `ex.req.<SENDER>`         | request / reply      |
9	| Market data          | `ex.md.<FEED>.<SENDER>`   | publish (JetStream)  |
10	| Best bid/offer        | `ex.bbo.<FEED>`           | publish (JetStream)  |
11	| Instrument metadata  | KV bucket `EX_META`       | JetStream KV         |
12	
13	- A **`<FEED>`** (feedcode) is 4 chars: 2 for the underlying + 2 for the expiry,
14	  e.g. `AAH6`. All feedcodes on the same underlying share the prefix (`AA`).
15	- A **`<SENDER>`** is an 8-char identity tag you choose for your strategy.
16	- An **order id** is 8 chars you choose, unique per sender. Publicly an order is
17	  identified by the 17-char `sender:orderid`.
18	- Prices and volumes are integers.
19	
20	## Order entry — `ex.req.<SENDER>` (request/reply)
21	
22	Publish your request to **`ex.req.<your-sender>`** (NATS request/reply; the reply
23	comes back on the inbox). The sender in the subject is what the exchange acts on,
24	and it **must match** the `<SENDER>` at the start of your message — you can only
25	enter and cancel orders for your own sender. Every request starts with your
26	`<SENDER>` then a 1-char type.
27	
28	| Type | Meaning      | Fields after `<SENDER> <type>`                                   |
29	|------|--------------|------------------------------------------------------------------|
30	| `A`  | add order    | `<FEED> <id:8> <B\|S> <volume> <price> <M\|L\|F>`                 |
31	| `C`  | cancel order | `<FEED> <id:8>`                                                  |
32	| `X`  | cancel many  | `<FEED> <B\|S\|X> <price>`  — see below                           |
33	| `Q`  | enable STP   | `<FEED>`                                                         |
34	| `W`  | disable STP  | `<FEED>`                                                          |
35	
36	Order types: **`L`** = limit (rests on the book), **`M`** = market, **`F`** =
37	fill-and-kill (executes what it can immediately, cancels the rest). Only `L`
38	rests.
39	
40	**Replies** start with the exchange's tag, then:
41	- `Y <n>` — accepted. For `A`, `<n>` is the volume that traded immediately. For
42	  `C`/`X`, `<n>` is how many orders were cancelled.
43	- `N <code> <text>` — rejected.
44	
45	Reject codes are stable numbers with a short text. A few you will hit early:
46	`100` malformed · `202` bad feedcode · `203` re-used order id. The rest are
47	yours to catalogue by experiment.
48	
49	### `X` — cancel many
50	
51	`X <FEED> <B|S|X> <price>` cancels several of **your** resting orders at once and
52	replies `Y <n>` with the number cancelled. The `<FEED>` may also select more than
53	one expiry, price may select more than one price
54	
55	## Market data — `ex.md.<FEED>.<SENDER>`
56	
57	Subscribe to `ex.md.<FEED>.*` to see all market data for a contract, or to
58	`ex.md.<FEED>.<your-sender>` for just your own. Every message is prefixed with an
59	`int64` nanosecond timestamp.
60	
61	| Type | Fields after `<ts> <type>`                                          |
62	|------|---------------------------------------------------------------------|
63	| `A`  | `<id:17> <B\|S> <volume> <price>`  — order added to the book          |
64	| `E`  | `<incoming:17> <resting:17> <volume> <price> <matchid> <B\|S>`        |
65	| `C`  | `<id:17>`  — order removed from the book                              |
66	| `T`  | same fields as `E`                                                    |
67	
68	- `E` is an **execution**. The trailing `B`/`S` is the **aggressor's side**. An
69	  order may fill over **several** `E` messages — accumulate them.
70	- `T` reports a **trade** — useful for watching trade flow.
71	- Your own fills show up on `ex.md.<FEED>.<your-sender>`.
72	
73	## Best bid/offer — `ex.bbo.<FEED>`
74	
75	`<ts> <FEED> <bid_px> <bid_vol> <ask_px> <ask_vol>`, with `-` for an empty side.
76	Updated as the top of book changes.
77	
78	## Instrument metadata — KV `EX_META`
79	
80	Key = feedcode; value is `key=value` pairs: `ticksize`, `ref_price`, `band`
81	(price band is `ref_price ± band`), `min_volume`, `max_volume`, `position_limit`,
82	`max_tps`, `last_traded_price`. Read it to learn each contract's tick, price
83	band, and limits.
84	
85	```bash
86	nats kv ls  EX_META
87	nats kv get EX_META AAH6
88	```
89	
90	## Probing
91	
92	The `nats` CLI is the fastest way to explore before you write code: `nats sub`
93	on the feeds above, `nats req` for order entry, `nats kv` for `EX_META`.
94

1	# Brings up NATS + the simulated exchange, a sample market, and your desk.
2	#
3	#   docker compose up                                   # NATS + exchange only
4	#   docker compose --profile sim up                     # + the sample market
5	#   docker compose --profile sim --profile strategy up  # + your desk (taker + quoter + hedger)
6	#
7	# All services share a private network and reach each other by name
8	# (the exchange's NATS is `nats://nats:4222`). NATS is also published to the host
9	# on :4222 for development (so the `nats` CLI works); your *desk* should talk
10	# to nats://nats:4222 from inside its containers, not to the host.
11	services:
12	  nats:
13	    image: nats:latest
14	    command: ["-js"]
15	    ports:
16	      - "4222:4222"

18	  exchange:
19	    image: sim-exchange:candidate

22	      NATS_URL: nats://nats:4222
23	      EX_CONFIG: /instruments.txt

28	      - nats

41	  # ---- the desk: three seats, three senders ------------------------------- #
42	  # Every seat container gets all three sender ids, so the seats can find each

53	      TAKER_RUN: "86400"

55	      - exchange
56	    restart: on-failure

58	      - strategy

1	#!/usr/bin/env bash
2	# Run the exchange, optionally with the sample market and/or your strategy.
3	#   ./run.sh                       exchange only
4	#   ./run.sh --sim                 + sample market
5	#   ./run.sh --strategy            + your strategy (from ./strategy)
6	#   ./run.sh --sim --strategy      everything (this is what grading runs)
7	set -e
8	cd "$(dirname "$0")"
9	./setup.sh
10	profiles=()
11	for a in "$@"; do
12	  case "$a" in
13	    --sim)      profiles+=(--profile sim) ;;
14	    --strategy) profiles+=(--profile strategy) ;;
15	    *) echo "unknown option: $a" >&2; exit 1 ;;
16	  esac
17	done
18	# --build: always rebuild from source so edits take effect (compose would
19	# otherwise happily reuse a stale image built from older code).
20	exec docker compose "${profiles[@]}" up --build
21

[tool result]
1	#!/usr/bin/env bash
2	# Ensures the simulated-exchange image is available locally.
3	set -e
4	cd "$(dirname "$0")"
5	if docker image inspect sim-exchange:candidate >/dev/null 2>&1; then
6	  exit 0
7	fi
8	if [ -f exchange/image.tar.xz ]; then
9	  if ! command -v xz >/dev/null 2>&1; then
10	    echo "ERROR: 'xz' is required to unpack the exchange image (install xz-utils)." >&2
11	    exit 1
12	  fi
13	  echo "Loading exchange image from exchange/image.tar.xz ..."
14	  xz -dc exchange/image.tar.xz | docker load
15	elif [ -f exchange/image.tar.gz ]; then
16	  echo "Loading exchange image from exchange/image.tar.gz ..."
17	  gunzip -c exchange/image.tar.gz | docker load
18	else
19	  echo "ERROR: 'sim-exchange:candidate' image not present and exchange/image.tar.xz is missing." >&2
20	  echo "Ask the task organisers for the exchange image." >&2
21	  exit 1
22	fi

[tool result]
1	# Exchange changelog
2	
3	
4	## v2.4 — 2026-05
5	
6	- Multi-shard matching: instruments are partitioned across matching shards
7	  (`EX_SHARDS`). Ordering guarantees are per-instrument, as before.
8	- Hot-path allocation removed; per-event latency counters added.
9	- Minor perf imperoments using boost containers
10	
11	## v2.3 — 2026-02
12	
13	- Matching: `F` (fill-and-kill) orders now execute atomically — they fill in
14	  full or reject. Partial executions no longer occur; internal strategies have
15	  been simplified accordingly.
16	- Per-feed transaction rate limiting (`max_tps`, reject codes `306`/`307`).
17	  Exceeding rate will disconnect user
18	
19	## v2.2 — 2025-11
20	
21	- Self-trade prevention: per-feed opt-in via `Q`, opt-out via `W`.
22	- `X` (cancel-many) accepted across instrument groups.
23	- split `E` into `E` and `T`
24	
25	## v2.1 — 2025-09
26	
27	- Market data and best-bid/offer moved to JetStream (`ex.md.<feed>.<sender>`,

[tool result]
1	# Python taker strategy
2	
3	The desk's existing momentum taker. It subscribes to the best-bid/offer feed
4	for one contract and, when the mid-price moves by `TAKER_THRESH` over the last
5	`TAKER_LAG` updates, crosses the spread with a fill-and-kill (`F`) order to
6	follow the move. It keeps its own position and mark-to-market PnL and
7	publishes them once a second on `strat.<sender>.status` (and to stdout).
8	
9	In the full stack (`./run.sh --sim --strategy`) it runs as a container — the
10	desk's taker seat, trading the front month all session. The instructions below
11	are for running it by hand while probing.
12	
13	## Run
14	
15	Bring up the exchange + sample market first (`./run.sh --sim`), then:
16	
17	```bash
18	python3 -m venv .venv && .venv/bin/pip install nats-py     # once
19	TAKER_FEED=<feed> .venv/bin/python taker/taker.py          # pick a listed feed
20	```
21	
22	(`nats kv ls EX_META` lists the feeds; see `PROTOCOL.md`.)
23	
24	Watch its self-reported state:
25	
26	```bash
27	nats sub 'strat.PYTKR001.status'
28	```
29	
30	## Config (env)
31	
32	| Var           | Default | Meaning                                        |
33	|---------------|---------|------------------------------------------------|
34	| `TAKER_FEED`  | `BTH6`  | contract to trade                              |
35	| `TAKER_SENDER`| `PYTKR001` | 8-char sender tag                           |
36	| `TAKER_CLIP`  | `3`     | order size per trade                           |
37	| `TAKER_MAX_POS`| `30`   | max absolute position                          |
38	| `TAKER_THRESH`| `10`    | mid move (price units) that triggers a trade   |
39	| `TAKER_LAG`   | `5`     | BBO updates back the move is measured over     |
40	| `TAKER_RUN`   | `20`    | seconds to run                                 |

Fair — let's do it hands-on. Two concepts first, then real commands.

**NATS** is a message bus. Everything is a *subject* (a topic name like `ex.bbo.AAH6`). Three verbs matter: `nats sub <subject>` listens, `nats pub` fires and forgets, `nats req` sends and waits for a reply. `nats sub` needs to know *what* to listen to — that's the error you hit.

**Docker Compose** starts the containers described in [docker-compose.yml](docker-compose.yml). `up` runs them in your terminal; `up -d` runs them in the background so you get your prompt back for probing.

Let me bring up the empty exchange:

**What happened.** `nats req` sent `PROBE001 A AAH6 ORD00001 B 10 595 L` to subject `ex.req.PROBE001` — sender, `A` for add, feed, order id, Buy, 10 lots, price 595, `L` for limit-rests-on-book. ASSISTANT  2026-08-14 23:26:46
==============================================================================
`Y 10` — it filled 10 of 25 and killed the rest. **Partial fills happen.** [CHANGELOG.md:14-16](CHANGELOG.md#L14) claims `F` became atomic in v2.3 ("fill in full or reject"); that is false against this build. The second `F` returned `Y 0` because the first one had eaten the whole bid. Trusting the changelog there would have put a silent hole in your hedger.

Let me catalogue the reject codes — the ones [PROTOCOL.md:47](PROTOCOL.md#L47) leaves as an exercise:

  "file_path": "/Users/emilygibbons/candidate/NOTES.md",
  "content": "# Notes\n\n## Session 1 — probing the exchange before writing anything\n\nMethod: brought up `docker compose up -d` with **no** `--sim`, so the book starts\nempty and every effect is something I caused. Listened on `ex.md.AAH6.*` and\n`ex.bbo.AAH6` with `nats sub` in the background, sent orders with `nats req`, and\nread replies plus the resulting market data. Findings below are all reproducible\nagainst the shipped image.\n\n### Confirmed by experiment\n\n**1. `F` (fill-and-kill) fills partially. The changelog is wrong.**\n`CHANGELOG.md` v2.3 claims `F` orders \"fill in full or reject\" and that partial\nexecutions no longer occur. Against a resting bid of 10 @ 595, an `F` sell of 25\nreplied `Y 10` — it traded 10 and killed the remaining 15. `PROTOCOL.md:37` is the\naccurate description. Anything that assumes an `F` is all-or-nothing (a hedger\nfiring a clip and assuming it's flat) will silently carry residual risk.\n\n**2. Order ids are consumed permanently, per sender — cancelling does not free them.**\n\n```\nA AAH6 RECYC001 B 5 585 L   -> Y 0     (rests)\nC AAH6 RECYC001             -> Y 1     (cancelled)\nA AAH6 RECYC001 B 5 585 L   -> N 203 re-used order id\n```\n\nA quoter that re-quotes on every BBO update burns ids continuously, so it needs a\nmonotonic id generator, never a recycled pool. 8 chars is plenty of space\n(base36 counter), but the generator has to survive for the whole session.\n\n**3. `X` (cancel-many) does not reliably select by side+price. Do not depend on it.**\nWith two of my own orders resting at 585, `X AAH6 B 585` replied `Y 0`; cancelling\neach by id immediately afterwards replied `Y 1` each, proving they were resting and\nmine. Other prices did cancel (`X AAH6 B 590` -> `Y 1`, `X AAH6 X 611` -> `Y 3`), so\nit is inconsistent rather than simply unimplemented. I could not derive the rule\nfrom the reply codes. **Design decision: cancel by explicit order id everywhere.**\nCost is one request per resting order, which is acceptable given `max_tps=0` locally\n(see open questions).\n\nFeed wildcards: `AA??` is accepted (never `N 100`) but matched nothing in my tests;\n`AA*`, `AA`, and `*` are all rejected `100 malformed`. So the wildcard char is `?`,\nbut I have no positive confirmation it ever selects.\n\n**4. Reject-code catalogue** (`PROTOCOL.md` documents only 100/202/203):\n\n| Code | Text                | Triggered by                                  |\n|------|---------------------|-----------------------------------------------|\n| 100  | malformed request   | too few fields, volume 0, order id != 8 chars |\n| 200  | bad sender identity | msg sender != sender in the subject           |\n| 202  | bad feedcode        | unlisted feed                                 |\n| 203  | re-used order id    | any id used before, even if cancelled         |\n| 204  | bad order type      | type not in `M`/`L`/`F`                       |\n| 205  | bad side            | side not in `B`/`S`                           |\n| 206  | orderid not used    | cancelling an id that was never added         |\n| 300  | volume too high     | volume > `max_volume`                         |\n| 302  | price out of range  | price outside `ref_price ± band`              |\n\n`306`/`307` (rate limiting, per the changelog) not yet reached — see below.\n\n**5. Negative prices are legal.** `B 10 -50 L` was accepted (`Y 0`). The band is\n`ref_price ± band` = 600 ± 5000, so the valid range is -4400..5600 and -50 sits\ninside it. Not a bug, but a quoter that assumes price > 0 when computing a spread\naround a low mid could send something it did not intend.\n\n### Open questions\n\n- **`max_tps=0`** in `exchange/instruments.txt`. Unknown whether 0 means unlimited\n  or no transactions permitted (empirically it is not blocking me, so: unlimited\n  locally). The changelog says exceeding the rate **disconnects the user**, which\n  is a session-ending event, not a reject to retry. The grading market is not this\n  market and may well set a real limit, so both seats need self-imposed rate\n  limiting regardless of what the local file says.\n- **`X` selector semantics** — unresolved, see above. Working around it rather than\n  solving it.\n- **STP** — `CHANGELOG` v2.0 says on by default, v2.2 says per-feed opt-in via `Q`.\n  Not yet tested. Matters because three of our seats quote and take on one book;\n  need to know whether our own seats can trade with each other, and whether that\n  shows up as desk position moving with no net exposure change.\n- **Position limits**: local `position_limit` is 1e9, effectively unlimited. The\n  grading market will differ; do not build anything that relies on the limit being\n  generous.\n\n### Environment note\n\nDeveloping on Apple Silicon (arm64); the exchange image is `linux/amd64` and runs\nemulated, so local latency numbers are not representative. Grading runs amd64\nnatively. Anything I conclude about *timing* here needs re-checking before I trust it.\n"

ASSISTANT  2026-08-14 23:32:07
==============================================================================
Book is clean, exchange restarted, and the only change in your repo is [NOTES.md](NOTES.md).

**The three commands you actually need**

```bash
docker compose up -d              # start exchange in background, get prompt back
docker compose ps                 # what's running
docker compose logs exchange      # what it's saying
nats sub 'ex.bbo.AAH6'            # watch top-of-book (Ctrl-C to stop)
nats req 'ex.req.PROBE001' 'PROBE001 A AAH6 ORD00001 B 10 595 L'
```

That last one is the whole order-entry protocol: *sender, `A` for add, feed, 8-char id, Buy/Sell, volume, price, `L`imit / `M`arket / `F`ill-and-kill.* The subject must end in the same sender name as the message starts with, or you get `200 bad sender identity`.

**What the probing turned up** — four things that change how you'd build the seats:

1. **`F` orders fill partially.** [CHANGELOG.md:14](CHANGELOG.md#L14) says they became all-or-nothing in v2.3; they did not. An `F` sell of 25 into a bid of 10 returns `Y 10`. A hedger that fires a clip and assumes it's flat would carry silent residual risk.
2. **Order ids are burned permanently, even after a cancel.** Re-using one gives `203`. Your quoter needs a monotonic id generator that lasts the session.
3. **`X` (cancel-many) is unreliable.** I had two orders resting at 585; `X AAH6 B 585` reported zero cancelled, then cancelling each by id worked. Other prices did work, so it's inconsistent, not unimplemented. I'd cancel by explicit id and not use `X`.
4. **Reject codes 100–302 catalogued** — the table's in NOTES.md.

Open and worth knowing before you design: `max_tps=0` locally means no rate limit *here*, but the changelog says breaching it **disconnects you**, and the grading market is not this market — so both seats should rate-limit themselves regardless.

I wrote all of that up with the evidence in [NOTES.md](NOTES.md), since the reasoning is half of what's graded.

Next I'd run `./run.sh --sim` and watch how the sample market and the legacy taker actually behave — that's what sets your quote width. Want me to do that, or go straight to scaffolding the Go quoter?

  "content": "#!/usr/bin/env bash\n# Live, readable view of one contract: top-of-book changes and trades.\n#\n#   tools/watch.sh            # AAH6\n#   tools/watch.sh AAM6\n#\n# Needs the `nats` CLI and the exchange running (docker compose --profile sim up -d).\n# Prints a line only when the top of book actually CHANGES -- the raw feed\n# republishes on every book event, so an unfiltered `nats sub` is mostly noise.\nset -euo pipefail\nFEED=\"${1:-AAH6}\"\nexport NATS_URL=\"${NATS_URL:-nats://localhost:4222}\"\n\nprintf '%-12s  %18s  %5s  %s\\n' \"time(utc)\" \"book\" \"spr\" \"event\"\nprintf -- '---------------------------------------------------------------\\n'\n\n{\n  nats sub --raw \"ex.bbo.${FEED}\"    | sed -u 's/^/BBO /' &\n  nats sub --raw \"ex.md.${FEED}.*\"   | sed -u 's/^/MD /'  &\n  wait\n} | awk -v feed=\"$FEED\" '\n  # ns timestamps exceed double precision, so slice the string instead of doing maths on it\n  function clock(ns,   s, hh, mm, ss) {\n    s  = substr(ns, 1, 10) + 0\n    hh = int(s / 3600) % 24; mm = int(s / 60) % 60; ss = s % 60\n    return sprintf(\"%02d:%02d:%02d.%s\", hh, mm, ss, substr(ns, 11, 3))\n  }\n  $1 == \"BBO\" {\n    # BBO <ts> <feed> <bid_px> <bid_vol> <ask_px> <ask_vol>, \"-\" for an empty side\n    bp = $4; bv = $5; ap = $6; av = $7\n    line = sprintf(\"%6s x%-5s %6s x%-5s\", bp, bv, ap, av)\n    if (line == last) next                    # unchanged top of book: skip\n    last = line\n    spr = (bp == \"-\" || ap == \"-\") ? \"--\" : ap - bp\n    printf \"%-12s  %18s  %5s\\n\", clock($2), line, spr\n    fflush()\n  }\n  $1 == \"MD\" && $3 == \"T\" {\n    # MD <ts> T <incoming:17> <resting:17> <volume> <price> <matchid> <B|S>\n    printf \"%-12s  %18s  %5s  TRADE %4s @ %-6s (%s aggressed)\\n\", \\\n           clock($2), \"\", \"\", $6, $7, ($9 == \"B\" ? \"buyer\" : \"seller\")\n    fflush()\n  }\n'\n"

ASSISTANT  2026-08-14 23:42:32
==============================================================================
Watcher works. Run it yourself:

```bash
docker compose --profile sim up -d     # market running in background
tools/watch.sh                         # AAH6, Ctrl-C to stop
tools/watch.sh AAM6                    # any listed feed
```

Sample of what you get — book on the left, spread, trades interleaved:

```
23:41:50.433                             TRADE    5 @ 443    (buyer aggressed)
23:41:50.433     440 x10       444 x8          4
23:41:50.585                             TRADE   10 @ 440    (seller aggressed)
23:41:50.585     439 x10       440 x5          1
23:41:50.886     438 x5        440 x5          2
```

It's in [tools/watch.sh](tools/watch.sh) — one subscription on `ex.>`, filtered to your feed, printing a line only when the top of book actually changes. Two things I had to fix while writing it, both worth knowing since they'll bite you in your own code: two `nats sub` processes writing to one pipe interleave mid-line and corrupt each other, and `nats sub` emits a blank line after every message body that will parse as a bogus update if you don't skip it.

**What the market actually looks like** — this is the part that matters for your design. Spread is typically 1–5 ticks with 5–15 lots a side, and trades arrive in bursts from both directions. But watch the mid over the three captures I took, roughly two minutes apart: **622 → 560 → 454 → 440**. The sample market wanders enormously relative to its own spread — you can earn 2 ticks quoting and lose 100 holding the inventory.

That's the whole argument for the task's structure. A quoter that just rests symmetric two-sided quotes will get run over by the drift; the money is in capturing spread while pushing inventory back to zero fast — skewing quotes against your position, and the hedger cutting anything that accumulates. It also means the BBO republish spam is a real hazard: re-quoting on every message would burn order ids and TPS for nothing, so you want to act only on genuine top-of-book changes, exactly like the dedupe in the watcher.

I'd note the drift observation in [NOTES.md](NOTES.md) — want me to add it, and then scaffold the Go quoter?

[tool result]
1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
2	"""Momentum taker strategy.
3	
4	Watches the best-bid/offer for one contract and, when the mid-price moves far
5	enough in one direction, crosses the spread with a fill-and-kill order to follow
6	the move. Tracks its own position and mark-to-market PnL and reports them once a
7	second on `strat.<sender>.status`.
8	
9	Config (env):
10	  NATS_URL        default nats://127.0.0.1:4222
11	  TAKER_FEED      contract to trade            (default BTH6)
12	  TAKER_SENDER    8-char sender tag            (default PYTKR001)
13	  TAKER_CLIP      order size per trade         (default 3)
14	  TAKER_MAX_POS   max absolute position        (default 30)
15	  TAKER_THRESH    mid move (price units) that triggers a trade (default 10)
16	  TAKER_LAG       how many BBO updates back the move is measured (default 5)
17	  TAKER_RUN       seconds to run               (default 20)
18	"""
19	import asyncio
20	import collections
21	import os
22	import random
23	
24	import nats
25	
26	NATS_URL = os.environ.get("NATS_URL", "nats://127.0.0.1:4222")
27	FEED = os.environ.get("TAKER_FEED", "BTH6")
28	SENDER = os.environ.get("TAKER_SENDER", "PYTKR001")
29	CLIP = int(os.environ.get("TAKER_CLIP", "3"))
30	MAX_POS = int(os.environ.get("TAKER_MAX_POS", "30"))
31	THRESH = int(os.environ.get("TAKER_THRESH", "10"))
32	LAG = int(os.environ.get("TAKER_LAG", "5"))
33	RUN_S = float(os.environ.get("TAKER_RUN", "20"))
34	
35	
36	class Taker:
37	    def __init__(self, nc):
38	        self.nc = nc
39	        self.oid = random.randint(0, 80_000_000)  # avoid id clashes across runs
40	        self.best_bid = None
41	        self.best_ask = None
42	        self.mids = collections.deque(maxlen=LAG + 1)
43	        self.position = 0
44	        self.cash = 0.0       # signed: buys spend cash, sells receive cash
45	        self.fills = 0
46	        self.send_lock = asyncio.Lock()
47	
48	    def next_oid(self):
49	        self.oid += 1
50	        return f"{self.oid:08d}"
51	
52	    def mid(self):
53	        if self.best_bid is None or self.best_ask is None:
54	            return None
55	        return (self.best_bid + self.best_ask) / 2
56	
57	    async def on_bbo(self, msg):
58	        # payload: "<ts> <FEED> <bid_px> <bid_vol> <ask_px> <ask_vol>", '-' if empty
59	        f = msg.data.decode().split()
60	        if len(f) < 6:
61	            return
62	        self.best_bid = None if f[2] == "-" else int(f[2])
63	        self.best_ask = None if f[4] == "-" else int(f[4])
64	        m = self.mid()
65	        if m is None:
66	            return
67	        self.mids.append(m)
68	        await self.maybe_trade()
69	
70	    async def maybe_trade(self):
71	        if len(self.mids) < self.mids.maxlen:
72	            return
73	        past, now = self.mids[0], self.mids[-1]
74	        if now - past >= THRESH and self.position < MAX_POS and self.best_ask is not None:
75	            await self.take("B")
76	        elif past - now >= THRESH and self.position > -MAX_POS and self.best_bid is not None:
77	            await self.take("S")
78	
79	    async def take(self, side):
80	        async with self.send_lock:
81	            px = self.best_ask if side == "B" else self.best_bid
82	            oid = self.next_oid()
83	            order = f"{SENDER} A {FEED} {oid} {side} {CLIP} {px} F"
84	            try:
85	                reply = await self.nc.request("ex.req", order.encode(), timeout=1.0)
86	            except Exception:
87	                return  # timed out; treat as no fill
88	            parts = reply.data.decode().split()
89	            # parts looks like ["EXCHANGE", "Y", "<n>"] on accept or
90	            # ["EXCHANGE", "N", "<code>", ...] on reject. parts[2] is just an
91	            # exchange-side detail field; for accepted orders we don't need it.
92	            if len(parts) >= 2 and parts[1] == "Y":
93	                # Fill-and-kill orders are marketable: they cross the spread and
94	                # fill in full against the resting book, so the size we asked for
95	                # is the size we got.
96	                filled = CLIP
97	                self.apply_fill(side, filled, px)
98	                self.fills += 1
99	
100	    def apply_fill(self, side, qty, px):
101	        signed = qty if side == "B" else qty
102	        self.position += signed
103	        self.cash -= signed * px
104	
105	    def pnl(self):
106	        m = self.mid()
107	        return self.cash + (self.position * m if m is not None else 0)
108	
109	    async def publish_status(self):
110	        s = (f"pos={self.position} cash={self.cash:.0f} "
111	             f"pnl={self.pnl():.0f} fills={self.fills}")
112	        print(f"[taker] {s}", flush=True)
113	        await self.nc.publish(f"strat.{SENDER}.status", s.encode())
114	
115	    async def reporter(self):
116	        while True:
117	            await asyncio.sleep(1.0)
118	            await self.publish_status()
119	
120	
121	async def main():
122	    nc = await nats.connect(NATS_URL)
123	    t = Taker(nc)
124	    await nc.subscribe(f"ex.bbo.{FEED}", cb=t.on_bbo)
125	    print(f"[taker] {SENDER} trading {FEED} clip={CLIP} thresh={THRESH} "
126	          f"for {RUN_S}s", flush=True)
127	    rep = asyncio.create_task(t.reporter())
128	    await asyncio.sleep(RUN_S)
129	    rep.cancel()
130	    await t.publish_status()
131	    print(f"[taker] final: position={t.position} pnl={t.pnl():.0f} "
132	          f"fills={t.fills}", flush=True)
133	    await nc.drain()
134	
135	
136	if __name__ == "__main__":
137	    asyncio.run(main())
138

ASSISTANT  2026-08-14 23:44:00
==============================================================================
Confirmed — **the taker has never traded a single lot.** It sends orders to bare `ex.req` ([taker.py:85](taker/taker.py#L85)); the exchange listens on `ex.req.>`, which doesn't match, so every request gets "No responders", hits the bare `except: return` at [line 86-87](taker/taker.py#L86-L87), and is silently swallowed. It has been reporting `pos=0 pnl=0` and looking healthy the whole time.

Two more, both live at [taker.py:100-103](taker/taker.py#L100-L103):

```python
signed = qty if side == "B" else qty     # both branches identical — sells add to position
```

and [line 96](taker/taker.py#L96) hardcodes `filled = CLIP` while dismissing `parts[2]` as "just an exchange-side detail field" — that field *is* the traded volume, and we proved `F` fills partially. So the moment you fix the subject, its self-reported position goes wrong in two directions at once.

**That decides a hedger design question:** never trust a seat's self-reported `strat.<sender>.status`. Derive every seat's position from the exchange's own `ex.md.<FEED>.<sender>` fill feed, which is ground truth.

Let me record all this, then build the quoter:

  "new_string": "### What the sample market looks like\n\nWatched with `tools/watch.sh` (written for this — one subscription on `ex.>`,\nfiltered to a feed, prints only genuine top-of-book changes).\n\nSpread is typically 1-5 ticks with 5-15 lots a side, and trades arrive in bursts\nfrom both directions. The important number is the drift: across three captures a\ncouple of minutes apart the mid went **622 -> 560 -> 454 -> 440**. The market\nwanders enormously relative to its own spread — you can earn 2 ticks quoting and\nlose 100 holding the inventory.\n\nThat sets the whole design. Symmetric two-sided quotes left resting through a move\nlike that lose far more to inventory than they earn in spread, so the quoter has to\nskew against its position and the hedger has to cut accumulated exposure quickly.\n\nAlso: the BBO feed republishes on **every book event**, not only when the top\nchanges — I logged dozens of identical `622 5 625 13` messages microseconds apart.\nRe-quoting on every BBO message would burn order ids (which are finite per sender,\nsee above) and TPS for nothing. Act on real top-of-book changes only.\n\n### The taker is broken (three bugs)\n\n`taker/` is \"legacy code; you own it now\", and it does not work at all.\n\n1. **It has never traded.** `taker.py:85` sends orders to bare `ex.req`; the\n   exchange listens on `ex.req.>`, which does not match a subject with no trailing\n   token. Verified directly: `nats req ex.req ...` -> *\"No responders are\n   available\"*, while `nats req ex.req.PYTKR001 ...` -> `Y 1`. The bare\n   `except: return` on the next line swallows the timeout, so it reports\n   `pos=0 pnl=0` and looks healthy.\n2. **Sells increase its position.** `taker.py:101` is\n   `signed = qty if side == \"B\" else qty` — both branches identical, so a sell is\n   booked as a buy. Position and cash are both wrong the moment it trades.\n3. **It assumes `F` fills in full.** `taker.py:96` hardcodes `filled = CLIP` and the\n   comment dismisses `parts[2]` as \"just an exchange-side detail field\". That field\n   is the traded volume, and `F` demonstrably fills partially (finding 1). Fixing\n   only the subject would give it silently wrong positions.\n\n**Design consequence for the hedger:** do not trust any seat's self-reported\n`strat.<sender>.status`. Positions get derived from the exchange's own\n`ex.md.<FEED>.<sender>` fill feed, which is ground truth and cannot drift from\nwhatever a seat believes about itself. This is worth doing even with a correct\ntaker — a hedger that depends on its siblings' bookkeeping inherits their bugs.\n\n### Environment note"

  "content": "package main\n\nimport (\n\t\"fmt\"\n\t\"strconv\"\n\t\"strings\"\n\t\"sync\"\n\t\"time\"\n\n\t\"github.com/nats-io/nats.go\"\n)\n\n// reply is a parsed exchange response: \"<TAG> Y <n>\" or \"<TAG> N <code> <text>\".\ntype reply struct {\n\tok   bool\n\tn    int // for A: volume traded immediately; for C/X: orders cancelled\n\tcode int\n\ttext string\n}\n\nfunc (r reply) String() string {\n\tif r.ok {\n\t\treturn fmt.Sprintf(\"Y %d\", r.n)\n\t}\n\treturn fmt.Sprintf(\"N %d %s\", r.code, r.text)\n}\n\n// client owns order entry for one sender: id generation, self-imposed rate\n// limiting, and reply parsing.\ntype client struct {\n\tnc     *nats.Conn\n\tsender string\n\tfeed   string\n\tsubj   string // ex.req.<sender> -- the exchange listens on ex.req.>, and a\n\t// request to bare \"ex.req\" silently gets no responders (the bug in taker.py).\n\n\tmu       sync.Mutex\n\tnextID   uint64\n\tinterval time.Duration // minimum gap between requests\n\tlast     time.Time\n}\n\nfunc newClient(nc *nats.Conn, sender, feed string, maxTPS int) *client {\n\tinterval := time.Duration(0)\n\tif maxTPS > 0 {\n\t\tinterval = time.Second / time.Duration(maxTPS)\n\t}\n\treturn &client{\n\t\tnc:     nc,\n\t\tsender: sender,\n\t\tfeed:   feed,\n\t\tsubj:   \"ex.req.\" + sender,\n\t\t// Order ids are consumed permanently per sender -- cancelling does not\n\t\t// free them (reject 203). Seeding from the wall clock means a container\n\t\t// restart resumes above every id the previous run burned. Milliseconds\n\t\t// since epoch is ~1.8e12, comfortably inside the 36^8 = 2.8e12 that fits\n\t\t// in the protocol's 8 characters (good until ~2059).\n\t\tnextID:   uint64(time.Now().UnixMilli()),\n\t\tinterval: interval,\n\t}\n}\n\nconst base36 = \"0123456789abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz\"\n\n// newOrderID returns a fresh 8-char id. Monotonic, never recycled.\nfunc (c *client) newOrderID() string {\n\tc.nextID++\n\tn := c.nextID\n\tvar b [8]byte\n\tfor i := 7; i >= 0; i-- {\n\t\tb[i] = base36[n%36]\n\t\tn /= 36\n\t}\n\treturn string(b[:])\n}\n\n// request rate-limits, sends, and parses. Exceeding the exchange's per-feed\n// max_tps disconnects the sender outright (changelog v2.3) rather than merely\n// rejecting, so we throttle ourselves regardless of what the local\n// instruments file says the limit is.\nfunc (c *client) request(msg string) (reply, error) {\n\tc.mu.Lock()\n\tif c.interval > 0 {\n\t\tif wait := c.interval - time.Since(c.last); wait > 0 {\n\t\t\ttime.Sleep(wait)\n\t\t}\n\t\tc.last = time.Now()\n\t}\n\tc.mu.Unlock()\n\n\tm, err := c.nc.Request(c.subj, []byte(msg), time.Second)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn reply{}, fmt.Errorf(\"request %q: %w\", msg, err)\n\t}\n\treturn parseReply(string(m.Data))\n}\n\nfunc parseReply(s string) (reply, error) {\n\tf := strings.Fields(s)\n\tif len(f) < 2 {\n\t\treturn reply{}, fmt.Errorf(\"short reply %q\", s)\n\t}\n\tswitch f[1] {\n\tcase \"Y\":\n\t\tn := 0\n\t\tif len(f) >= 3 {\n\t\t\tn, _ = strconv.Atoi(f[2])\n\t\t}\n\t\treturn reply{ok: true, n: n}, nil\n\tcase \"N\":\n\t\tr := reply{}\n\t\tif len(f) >= 3 {\n\t\t\tr.code, _ = strconv.Atoi(f[2])\n\t\t}\n\t\tif len(f) >= 4 {\n\t\t\tr.text = strings.Join(f[3:], \" \")\n\t\t}\n\t\treturn r, nil\n\t}\n\treturn reply{}, fmt.Errorf(\"unparsable reply %q\", s)\n}\n\n// add places an order and returns its id and the volume that traded immediately.\nfunc (c *client) add(side byte, vol, px int, typ byte) (string, int, error) {\n\tid := c.newOrderID()\n\tmsg := fmt.Sprintf(\"%s A %s %s %c %d %d %c\", c.sender, c.feed, id, side, vol, px, typ)\n\tr, err := c.request(msg)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn \"\", 0, err\n\t}\n\tif !r.ok {\n\t\treturn \"\", 0, fmt.Errorf(\"add rejected: %s\", r)\n\t}\n\treturn id, r.n, nil\n}\n\n// cancel removes one resting order by id.\n//\n// Deliberately not using X (cancel-many): it does not reliably select by\n// side+price. Observed two of our own orders resting at 585 while\n// \"X AAH6 B 585\" reported 0 cancelled, and cancelling each by id immediately\n// afterwards worked. Per-id cancellation costs one request each, which the rate\n// limiter above absorbs.\nfunc (c *client) cancel(id string) error {\n\tr, err := c.request(fmt.Sprintf(\"%s C %s %s\", c.sender, c.feed, id))\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\treturn err\n\t}\n\t// 206 \"orderid not used\" means it is already gone (filled or cancelled);\n\t// that is the state we wanted, so it is not an error.\n\tif !r.ok && r.code != 206 {\n\t\treturn fmt.Errorf(\"cancel %s rejected: %s\", id, r)\n\t}\n\treturn nil\n}\n"
}

Payload: \"<ts> <FEED> <bid_px> <bid_vol> <ask_px> <ask_vol>\",\n// with \"-\" for an empty side.\nfunc (q *quoter) onBBO(m *nats.Msg) {\n\tf := strings.Fields(string(m.Data))\n\tif len(f) < 6 {\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\ttop := f[2] + \" \" + f[3] + \" \" + f[4] + \" \" + f[5]\n\n\tq.mu.Lock()\n\tif top == q.lastTop {\n\t\tq.mu.Unlock()\n\t\treturn // genuinely unchanged: ignore, or we churn ids and TPS for nothing\n\t}\n\tq.lastTop = top\n\tq.bidPx, q.bidOK = parsePx(f[2])\n\tq.askPx, q.askOK = parsePx(f[4])\n\tq.mu.Unlock()\n\n\tq.signal()\n}\n\nfunc parsePx(s string) (int, bool) {\n\tif s == \"-\" {\n\t\treturn 0, false\n\t}\n\tv, err := strconv.Atoi(s)\n\treturn v, err == nil\n}\n\n// onOwnMD consumes ex.md.<FEED>.<SENDER> -- our own order and fill events. This\n// is the authoritative position: it comes from the exchange, not from our own\n// belief about what our orders did.\nfunc (q *quoter) onOwnMD(m *nats.Msg) {\n\tf := strings.Fields(string(m.Data))\n\tif len(f) < 2 {\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\tswitch f[1] {\n\tcase \"E\":\n\t\t// <ts> E <incoming:17> <resting:17> <volume> <price> <matchid> <B|S>\n\t\t// Only E is handled: T reports the same trade again and would double-count.\n\t\tif len(f) < 8 {\n\t\t\treturn\n\t\t}\n\t\tvol, err1 := strconv.Atoi(f[4])\n\t\tpx, err2 := strconv.Atoi(f[5])\n\t\tif err1 != nil || err2 != nil {\n\t\t\treturn\n\t\t}\n\t\taggressor := f[7] // side of the incoming order, not necessarily ours\n\t\tvar side byte\n\t\tvar ourID string\n\t\tswitch {\n\t\tcase q.isOurs(f[2]):\n\t\t\tside = aggressor[0]\n\t\t\tourID = orderID(f[2])\n\t\tcase q.isOurs(f[3]):\n\t\t\tside = flip(aggressor[0]) // we were resting, so we took the other side\n\t\t\tourID = orderID(f[3])\n\t\tdefault:\n\t\t\treturn\n\t\t}\n\t\tq.applyFill(side, vol, px, ourID)\n\t\tq.signal()\n\tcase \"C\":\n\t\t// <ts> C <id:17> -- one of our orders left the book\n\t\tif len(f) >= 3 && q.isOurs(f[2]) {\n\t\t\tq.forget(orderID(f[2]))\n\t\t}\n\t}\n}\n\nfunc (q *quoter) isOurs(id17 string) bool { return strings.HasPrefix(id17, q.cfg.Sender+\":\") }\n\nfunc orderID(id17 string) string {\n\tif i := strings.IndexByte(id17, ':'); i >= 0 {\n\t\treturn id17[i+1:]\n\t}\n\treturn id17\n}\n\nfunc flip(side byte) byte {\n\tif side == 'B' {\n\t\treturn 'S'\n\t}\n\treturn 'B'\n}\n\nfunc (q *quoter) applyFill(side byte, vol, px int, id string) {\n\tq.mu.Lock()\n\tdefer q.mu.Unlock()\n\tif side == 'B' {\n\t\tq.position += vol\n\t\tq.cash -= int64(vol) * int64(px)\n\t} else {\n\t\tq.position -= vol\n\t\tq.cash += int64(vol) * int64(px)\n\t}\n\tq.fills++\n\t// Decrement the resting order this filled against. F orders fill partially\n\t// and so do resting limits, so a fill does not mean the order is gone.\n\tfor _, r := range []*resting{&q.bid, &q.ask} {\n\t\tif r.id == id {\n\t\t\tr.vol -= vol\n\t\t\tif r.vol <= 0 {\n\t\t\t\t*r = resting{}\n\t\t\t}\n\t\t}\n\t}\n\tlog.Printf(\"fill %c %d @ %d -> pos=%d\", side, vol, px, q.position)\n}\n\nfunc (q *quoter) forget(id string) {\n\tq.mu.Lock()\n\tdefer q.mu.Unlock()\n\tfor _, r := range []*resting{&q.bid, &q.ask} {\n\t\tif r.id == id {\n\t\t\t*r = resting{}\n\t\t}\n\t}\n}\n\n// target is the quote we want on one side; vol == 0 means \"do not quote\".\ntype target struct {\n\tpx  int\n\tvol int\n}\n\n// desired computes both sides from the current book and our inventory.\nfunc (q *quoter) desired() (bid, ask target, ok bool) {\n\tq.mu.Lock()\n\tdefer q.mu.Unlock()\n\n\tif !q.bidOK || !q.askOK || q.askPx <= q.bidPx {\n\t\treturn target{}, target{}, false // one-sided or crossed: no fair value\n\t}\n\n\tfair := float64(q.bidPx+q.askPx) / 2\n\t// Inventory skew: push both quotes away from the position we are carrying, so\n\t// the side that flattens us is the attractive one. At MaxPos the shift is a\n\t// full SkewTicks, which is what makes this a liquidity strategy rather than a\n\t// directional one -- the mid drifts far more than the spread pays.\n\tskew := q.cfg.SkewTicks * float64(q.position) / float64(q.cfg.MaxPos)\n\tbid.px = int(math.Round(fair - q.cfg.EdgeTicks - skew))\n\task.px = int(math.Round(fair + q.cfg.EdgeTicks - skew))\n\n\t// Stay passive. A limit order that crosses executes immediately, which would\n\t// make us the aggressor and pay the spread we are trying to earn.\n\tif bid.px >= q.askPx {\n\t\tbid.px = q.askPx - 1\n\t}\n\tif ask.px <= q.bidPx {\n\t\task.px = q.bidPx + 1\n\t}\n\n\t// Never quote a size that could take us through the position limit.\n\tbid.vol = clamp(q.cfg.Clip, 0, q.cfg.MaxPos-q.position)\n\task.vol = clamp(q.cfg.Clip, 0, q.cfg.MaxPos+q.position)\n\treturn bid, ask, true\n}\n\nfunc clamp(v, lo, hi int) int {\n\tif v < lo {\n\t\treturn lo\n\t}\n\tif v > hi {\n\t\treturn hi\n\t}\n\treturn v\n}\n\n// requoteLoop owns all order entry, so network calls never block the NATS\n// callbacks that feed us market data.\nfunc (q *quoter) requoteLoop(ctx context.Context) {\n\t// A slow tick refreshes quotes even when the top of book is static, so a\n\t// dropped reply or a missed event cannot leave us un-quoted indefinitely.\n\ttick := time.NewTicker(time.Second)\n\tdefer tick.Stop()\n\tfor {\n\t\tselect {\n\t\tcase <-ctx.Done():\n\t\t\treturn\n\t\tcase <-q.wake:\n\t\tcase <-tick.C:\n\t\t}\n\t\tq.requote()\n\t\tif q.cfg.MinRequote > 0 {\n\t\t\ttime.Sleep(q.cfg.MinRequote) // coalesce bursts of book updates\n\t\t}\n\t}\n}\n\nfunc (q *quoter) requote() {\n\tq.quoteMu.Lock()\n\tdefer q.quoteMu.Unlock()\n\n\tbid, ask, ok := q.desired()\n\tif !ok {\n\t\tq.cancelAll()\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\tq.reconcile(&q.bid, bid, 'B')\n\tq.reconcile(&q.ask, ask, 'S')\n}\n\n// reconcile brings one side to its target, leaving an already-correct order\n// alone so it keeps its place in the queue.\nfunc (q *quoter) reconcile(cur *resting, want target, side byte) {\n\tq.mu.Lock()\n\tnow := *cur\n\tq.mu.Unlock()\n\n\tif now.live() && now.px == want.px && now.vol == want.vol {\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\tif now.live() {\n\t\tif err := q.cl.cancel(now.id); err != nil {\n\t\t\tlog.Printf(\"cancel %s: %v\", now.id, err)\n\t\t\treturn // leave it recorded; we retry next cycle rather than orphan it\n\t\t}\n\t\tq.mu.Lock()\n\t\tif cur.id == now.id {\n\t\t\t*cur = resting{}\n\t\t}\n\t\tq.mu.Unlock()\n\t}\n\tif want.vol <= 0 {\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\tid, traded, err := q.cl.add(side, want.vol, want.px, 'L')\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tlog.Printf(\"add %c %d @ %d: %v\", side, want.vol, want.px, err)\n\t\treturn\n\t}\n\tq.mu.Lock()\n\t// Anything that traded on entry is reported on our md feed and booked there;\n\t// only the remainder is actually resting.\n\tif rem := want.vol - traded; rem > 0 {\n\t\t*cur = resting{id: id, px: want.px, vol: rem}\n\t}\n\tq.mu.Unlock()\n}\n\nfunc (q *quoter) cancelAll() {\n\tfor _, r := range []*resting{&q.bid, &q.ask} {\n\t\tq.mu.Lock()\n\t\tcur := *r\n\t\tq.mu.Unlock()\n\t\tif !cur.live() {\n\t\t\tcontinue\n\t\t}\n\t\tif err := q.cl.cancel(cur.id); err != nil {\n\t\t\tlog.Printf(\"cancel %s: %v\", cur.id, err)\n\t\t\tcontinue\n\t\t}\n\t\tq.mu.Lock()\n\t\tif r.id == cur.id {\n\t\t\t*r = resting{}\n\t\t}\n\t\tq.mu.Unlock()\n\t}\n}\n\n// status reports position and mark-to-market PnL once a second, mirroring the\n// taker's strat.<sender>.status convention.\nfunc (q *quoter) status() string {\n\tq.mu.Lock()\n\tdefer q.mu.Unlock()\n\tpnl := float64(q.cash)\n\tif q.bidOK && q.askOK {\n\t\tpnl += float64(q.position) * float64(q.bidPx+q.askPx) / 2\n\t}\n\treturn fmt.Sprintf(\"pos=%d cash=%d pnl=%.0f fills=%d bid=%s ask=%s\",\n\t\tq.position, q.cash, pnl, q.fills, fmtQuote(q.bid), fmtQuote(q.ask))\n}\n\nfunc fmtQuote(r resting) string {\n\tif !r.live() {\n\t\treturn \"-\"\n\t}\n\treturn fmt.Sprintf(\"%dx%d\", r.px, r.vol)\n}\n\nfunc (q *quoter) reportLoop(ctx context.Context) {\n\ttick := time.NewTicker(time.Second)\n\tdefer tick.Stop()\n\tfor {\n\t\tselect {\n\t\tcase <-ctx.Done():\n\t\t\treturn\n\t\tcase <-tick.C:\n\t\t\ts := q.status()\n\t\t\tlog.Print(s)\n\t\t\t_ = q.nc.Publish(\"strat.\"+q.cfg.Sender+\".status\", []byte(s))\n\t\t}\n\t}\n}\n"

  "content": "// Command quoter is the desk's market-making seat: it rests two-sided liquidity\n// on one contract and earns the spread, skewing its quotes against whatever\n// inventory it is carrying so it stays close to flat.\npackage main\n\nimport (\n\t\"context\"\n\t\"log\"\n\t\"os\"\n\t\"os/signal\"\n\t\"strconv\"\n\t\"syscall\"\n\t\"time\"\n\n\t\"github.com/nats-io/nats.go\"\n)\n\ntype config struct {\n\tNatsURL string\n\tSender  string\n\tFeed    string\n\n\tClip       int           // size per quote\n\tMaxPos     int           // hard cap on absolute position\n\tEdgeTicks  float64       // half-spread we try to earn, in ticks\n\tSkewTicks  float64       // quote shift at full inventory, in ticks\n\tMaxTPS     int           // self-imposed request rate cap\n\tMinRequote time.Duration // floor on time between requote cycles\n}\n\nfunc loadConfig() config {\n\treturn config{\n\t\tNatsURL: env(\"NATS_URL\", \"nats://127.0.0.1:4222\"),\n\t\t// Compose injects these; grading injects real values.\n\t\tSender: env(\"SENDER\", \"QUOTE001\"),\n\t\tFeed:   env(\"QUOTER_FEED\", env(\"TAKER_FEED\", \"AAH6\")),\n\n\t\tClip:       envInt(\"QUOTER_CLIP\", 5),\n\t\tMaxPos:     envInt(\"QUOTER_MAX_POS\", 25),\n\t\tEdgeTicks:  envFloat(\"QUOTER_EDGE\", 2),\n\t\tSkewTicks:  envFloat(\"QUOTER_SKEW\", 4),\n\t\tMaxTPS:     envInt(\"QUOTER_MAX_TPS\", 20),\n\t\tMinRequote: time.Duration(envInt(\"QUOTER_MIN_REQUOTE_MS\", 50)) * time.Millisecond,\n\t}\n}\n\nfunc env(k, def string) string {\n\tif v := os.Getenv(k); v != \"\" {\n\t\treturn v\n\t}\n\treturn def\n}\n\nfunc envInt(k string, def int) int {\n\tif v, err := strconv.Atoi(os.Getenv(k)); err == nil {\n\t\treturn v\n\t}\n\treturn def\n}\n\nfunc envFloat(k string, def float64) float64 {\n\tif v, err := strconv.ParseFloat(os.Getenv(k), 64); err == nil {\n\t\treturn v\n\t}\n\treturn def\n}\n\nfunc main() {\n\tlog.SetFlags(log.Ltime | log.Lmicroseconds)\n\tlog.SetPrefix(\"[quoter] \")\n\tcfg := loadConfig()\n\n\tnc, err := nats.Connect(cfg.NatsURL,\n\t\tnats.Name(\"quoter-\"+cfg.Sender),\n\t\tnats.MaxReconnects(-1), // the exchange may come up after us\n\t\tnats.ReconnectWait(time.Second),\n\t)\n\tif err != nil {\n\t\tlog.Fatalf(\"connect %s: %v\", cfg.NatsURL, err)\n\t}\n\tdefer nc.Close()\n\tlog.Printf(\"%s quoting %s clip=%d maxpos=%d edge=%.1f skew=%.1f\",\n\t\tcfg.Sender, cfg.Feed, cfg.Clip, cfg.MaxPos, cfg.EdgeTicks, cfg.SkewTicks)\n\n\tq := newQuoter(nc, cfg)\n\n\tif _, err := nc.Subscribe(\"ex.bbo.\"+cfg.Feed, q.onBBO); Not self-matching — 0 of 32 executions had us on both sides. So the round-trip at 650 was genuine trading, which makes it a strategy problem rather than a bug: our quotes are landing at prices with **no edge**.

**What's built and working** — [strategy/](strategy/), four files, builds clean and runs against the live market:

- [exchange.go](strategy/exchange.go) — order entry: monotonic base36 ids seeded from the wall clock (so a container restart resumes above every id the last run burned), self-imposed TPS throttle, reply parsing, cancel-by-id only.
- [quoter.go](strategy/quoter.go) — book state, position from our own `ex.md` fill feed, inventory-skewed quoting, cancel/replace that leaves an already-correct order alone to keep queue priority.
- [main.go](strategy/main.go) — env config, signal handling, pulls quotes on shutdown.
- [Dockerfile](strategy/Dockerfile) — multi-stage, builds from source, `CGO_ENABLED=0`.

Inventory control works: 24 fills in 15 seconds with position staying between -9 and +6 against a limit of 25, and it flattened itself repeatedly without help.

**It loses money as it stands** — about -270 over 15 seconds. Two causes, and I'd rather tell you than bury it:

1. **Zero-edge quotes.** My "stay passive" clamp (`quoter.go`, `desired()`) pins a quote to `bestAsk-1` / `bestBid+1` whenever the skewed target would cross. That keeps us passive but hands away the edge — hence buy 5 @ 650, sell 5 @ 650. The fix is a minimum-edge floor: refuse to quote a side at all rather than quote it for nothing.
2. **Adverse selection.** The mid moved 650 → 739 → 683 inside 15 seconds. Resting 2 ticks wide in a market that travels 90 means the fills you get are disproportionately the ones you didn't want.

Both point the same way, and it's a clean division of labour: **the quoter should never give up edge, and the hedger is the seat that's allowed to pay the spread deliberately to cut risk.**

Want me to put in the min-edge floor and re-measure, or move on to the hedger now that the quoter demonstrably holds a position under control?

ASSISTANT  2026-08-14 23:50:57

Fair enough — I jumped into jargon. Let me back up.

**What the quoter is doing.** Think of a shop that both buys and sells the same item. It posts two prices: *"I'll buy at 648, I'll sell at 652."* If one customer sells to it at 648 and another buys from it at 652, the shop pockets 4. It never bets on whether the item is going up or down — it just wants both sides to keep coming. That's what "providing two-sided liquidity" in the task means, and that 4 is what I've been calling **edge**.

Your quoter does exactly this, and the mechanics work. But it lost money for two reasons.

**Problem 1: it sold at the price it bought at.** From your run:

```
fill B 5 @ 650   ← bought 5 at 650
fill S 5 @ 650   ← sold those 5 at 650
```

Bought at 650, sold at 650, earned nothing. The shop is doing all the work for zero margin.

That's my bug, not bad luck. I wrote a rule saying "never post a buy price at or above the market's sell price" — sensible, because otherwise you instantly trade instead of waiting. But when that rule kicked in I made the code *shove the price to the nearest allowed value* rather than *decline to quote*. So it kept posting prices with no profit in them. The fix is to say: if we can't get our margin, don't post that side at all.

**Problem 2: the price ran away while it was holding.** The market went 650 → 739 → 683 in fifteen seconds. Say the shop buys an item at 650 and, before anyone buys it, the item is only worth 600. It's down 50 — twelve times the 4 it was trying to earn. The shop's margin is tiny; the price swings are huge. So the danger isn't the trading, it's *holding anything at all* for more than a moment.

**That's why the task has three separate programs.** Everything above is the argument for the split:

- The **quoter** posts both prices and should never sell itself short on margin — if there's no profit in a price, it stays out.
- The **hedger** exists because holding is dangerous. When the desk has accumulated a pile of something, the hedger's job is to get rid of it *fast*, and it's allowed to accept a bad price to do that — losing 2 to get flat beats losing 50 because you waited.
- The **taker** is the existing program that bets on direction. It's also broken, which we found earlier.

So "the quoter never gives up margin, the hedger is allowed to pay for speed" just means: one program is careful about price, the other is careful about time, and they'd be bad at each other's jobs.

Which part should I go deeper on — how the quoter decides its prices, why holding is the risk, or how the three programs fit together?

201	
202		fair := float64(q.bidPx+q.askPx) / 2
203		// Inventory skew: push both quotes away from the position we are carrying, so
204		// the side that flattens us is the attractive one. At MaxPos the shift is a
205		// full SkewTicks, which is what makes this a liquidity strategy rather than a
206		// directional one -- the mid drifts far more than the spread pays.
207		skew := q.cfg.SkewTicks * float64(q.position) / float64(q.cfg.MaxPos)
208		bid.px = int(math.Round(fair - q.cfg.EdgeTicks - skew))
209		ask.px = int(math.Round(fair + q.cfg.EdgeTicks - skew))
210	
211		// Stay passive. A limit order that crosses executes immediately, which would
212		// make us the aggressor and pay the spread we are trying to earn.
213		if bid.px >= q.askPx {
214			bid.px = q.askPx - 1
215		}
216		if ask.px <= q.bidPx {
217			ask.px = q.bidPx + 1
218		}
219	
220		// Never quote a size that could take us through the position limit.
221		bid.vol = clamp(q.cfg.Clip, 0, q.cfg.MaxPos-q.position)
222		ask.vol = clamp(q.cfg.Clip, 0, q.cfg.MaxPos+q.position)
223		return bid, ask, true
224	}

229		}

ASSISTANT  2026-08-14 23:53:50
==============================================================================
Here's the whole decision, in the order the code does it ([quoter.go:196-223](strategy/quoter.go#L196-L223)). Defaults: quote size 5, max position 25, edge 2, skew 4.

**Step 1 — what's it worth?** Take the market's best buy price and best sell price and use the midpoint. Market shows 648 bid / 652 ask, so fair value is **650**. If either side is missing, we don't quote at all — no midpoint, no opinion.

**Step 2 — step away from fair by the edge.** Buy 2 below, sell 2 above:

> bid **648**, ask **652**

If both fill, we bought at 648 and sold at 652: 4 per lot. That's the business.

**Step 3 — lean against what we're holding.** If we're already long, we want to sell more than we buy, so both prices shift *down*: our sell price gets cheaper (tempting), our buy price gets stingier (discouraging). The shift is proportional to how full we are — `skew × position ÷ max position`.

Here's the same market at different positions:

| Position | Shift | Our bid | Our ask | Margin vs fair (650) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0 | 0.0 | 648 | 652 | +2 / +2 |
| +10 | 1.6 | 646 | 650 | +4 / **0** |
| +20 | 3.2 | 645 | 649 | +5 / **−1** |

**And there's your losing trade.** At +10 we're offering to sell at 650 — exactly fair value, no margin. At +20 we're selling at 649, *below* what it's worth, losing 1 per lot on purpose. That's the `fill B 5 @ 650 / fill S 5 @ 650` pair from your run: buy at 650, get longer, skew pushes the sell price down onto 650, sell it back for nothing.

This is a straight parameter mistake I made: **I set skew (4) larger than edge (2)**. Since the shift reaches the full 4 at max position, it overtakes the 2 of margin once position passes about half the limit. Past that point the quoter is knowingly selling at a loss to get flat — doing the hedger's job, and doing it worse, because the hedger can clear a position in one trade while the quoter has to wait for someone to come to it.

The fix is a floor: cap the shift so it can never eat the whole margin. The quoter then always makes money on every fill, and when inventory builds past what leaning can fix, that's the hedger's problem by design.

**Step 4 — don't accidentally trade instantly.** If our buy price lands at or above the market's sell price, the exchange matches it on the spot and we've *paid* the spread instead of earning it. Lines 213-218 pull the price back inside. Necessary, but as I mentioned it currently pulls to the nearest legal price rather than declining to quote — the same "post something worthless" failure, from a different direction.

**Step 5 — size.** Never offer a quantity that could push us through the 25 limit. At +22, the buy side is cut to 3.

Want me to implement the fix — cap the skew below the edge, and skip a side entirely rather than quote it for nothing — and re-run so you can see whether the PnL turns around?

ASSISTANT  2026-08-14 23:56:41
==============================================================================
[strategy/quoter.go](strategy/quoter.go) is the strategy brain — 360 lines that boil down to *listen, remember, decide, act, report*. The other two files are plumbing: [exchange.go](strategy/exchange.go) knows how to phrase an order, [main.go](strategy/main.go) reads settings and wires things together.

**1. It listens to two things.**

`onBBO` ([:66](strategy/quoter.go#L66)) receives the market's best buy and sell prices. First thing it does is check whether they actually changed ([:71](strategy/quoter.go#L71)) — remember the feed republishes constantly with identical numbers, and reacting to those would burn order ids for nothing.

`onOwnMD` ([:97](strategy/quoter.go#L97)) receives our own trades, straight from the exchange. This is how it knows its position — not by remembering what it *asked* for, but by being told what actually happened. That's the lesson from the broken taker, which assumed its orders worked and was wrong on both counts.

There's a subtlety at [:110-124](strategy/quoter.go#L110-L124): a trade message says which side the *aggressor* was, not which side we were. If we were the one who crossed, our side is the aggressor's side. If we were sitting there and someone hit us, our side is the opposite. Getting that backwards would flip the sign of the position — the exact bug the taker has.

**2. It remembers five things** ([:25](strategy/quoter.go#L25)): the market's current prices, our position, our cash, and the two orders we currently have resting. `applyFill` ([:153](strategy/quoter.go#L153)) updates all of it on every trade — position up and cash down when buying, the reverse when selling — and reduces the resting order by however much filled, because a partial fill doesn't mean the order is gone.

**3. It decides** in `desired` ([:194](strategy/quoter.go#L194)) — the midpoint, step away by the edge, lean against inventory, don't accidentally cross, cap the size. That's the part we just walked through, including the skew-bigger-than-edge flaw.

**4. It acts** in `reconcile` ([:272](strategy/quoter.go#L272)), and this is the part worth understanding. It doesn't cancel and repost every cycle. It compares what's resting against what it wants, and **if they already match, it does nothing** ([:278](strategy/quoter.go#L278)). Two reasons: every repost burns an order id permanently, and orders are served in the order they arrived — cancelling and reposting the same price sends you to the back of the queue behind everyone who was patient.

All of this happens in one dedicated loop ([:238](strategy/quoter.go#L238)) rather than inside the market-data handlers, so waiting on the exchange to reply never blocks us from seeing prices. A one-second tick also re-checks even when nothing moves, so a dropped reply can't silently leave us with no quotes up.

**5. It reports** ([:330](strategy/quoter.go#L330)) once a second — position, cash, profit, quotes — to the console and to `strat.QUOTE001.status`, matching the taker's convention. That's the channel the hedger could listen to, though based on what we found, the hedger should read the exchange's fill feed directly instead of trusting anyone's self-report.

**What it deliberately doesn't do:** never uses `X` to cancel in bulk (unreliable), never posts a price that would trade instantly, never quotes a size that could breach the position limit, and never quotes at all when the market is one-sided.

The known flaw is still Step 3 — it quotes through fair value once inventory passes about half the limit. Want me to fix that and re-run?

210	
211		// Minimum-edge floor. Skew is allowed to make the side that flattens us more
212		// attractive, but never past the point where the fill stops being profitable:
213		// with SkewTicks > EdgeTicks the raw skew above quotes straight through fair
214		// value once the position passes MaxPos*EdgeTicks/SkewTicks, so we were paying
215		// to reduce inventory. Clearing inventory at a loss is the hedger's job, and it
216		// can do it in one trade instead of waiting for someone to come to us.
217		//
218		// Only the attractive side is floored. The discouraging side stays unbounded --
219		// quoting stingier than fair is always safe.
220		if cap := int(math.Floor(fair - q.cfg.MinEdgeTicks)); bid.px > cap {
221			bid.px = cap
222		}
223		if floor := int(math.Ceil(fair + q.cfg.MinEdgeTicks)); ask.px < floor {
224			ask.px = floor
225		}
226	
227		// Stay passive: a limit order that crosses executes immediately, which would
228		// make us the aggressor and pay the spread we are trying to earn. With a
229		// positive MinEdgeTicks the floor above already guarantees this (our bid sits
230		// below fair, which sits below the best ask), so this is a guard rather than a
231		// reprice -- if it ever fires, sitting the side out is right, since any price
232		// that satisfies it would breach the minimum edge.
233		if bid.px >= q.askPx {
234			bid.px, bid.vol = 0, 0
235		}
236		if ask.px <= q.bidPx {
237			ask.px, ask.vol = 0, 0
238		}
239	
240		// Never quote a size that could take us through the position limit.
241		bid.vol = clamp(q.cfg.Clip, 0, q.cfg.MaxPos-q.position)

Look at the first round trip:

```
23:59:59.572  fill B 5 @ 537
23:59:59.774  fill S 5 @ 496     ← 200ms later, 41 points lower
```

And at 00:00:04 it bought at 558 having quoted 494 one second earlier. The market is jumping tens of points in fractions of a second while we're trying to earn 2. That's not a floor problem — the floor was addressing the wrong thing. Let me look at what the price process actually is:

[tool result]
1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
2	"""Sample market for developing your strategy.
3	
4	A small set of synthetic participants that make the contracts on underlying "AA"
5	(front AAH6, backs AAM6, AAU6) behave like a real-ish market: background
6	liquidity on every month, casual takers, and a "mover" that walks the front-month
7	price around and occasionally sweeps the book aggressively. It is deliberately
8	simpler than what we grade against — use it to see your quoter work.
9	
10	Run it pointed at the exchange's NATS (see the project README). It runs until you
11	stop it. Env: NATS_URL (default nats://127.0.0.1:4222), SIM_SEED (default 1).
12	"""
13	import asyncio
14	import os
15	import random
16	import threading
17	import time
18	
19	import nats
20	
21	NATS_URL = os.environ.get("NATS_URL", "nats://127.0.0.1:4222")
22	SEED = int(os.environ.get("SIM_SEED", "1"))
23	
24	FRONT = "AAH6"
25	CONTRACTS = {"AAH6": {"rel": 0}, "AAM6": {"rel": 15}, "AAU6": {"rel": 30}}
26	ALL = list(CONTRACTS)
27	TICK = 1
28	START = 600
29	
30	
31	def now():
32	    return time.monotonic()
33	
34	
35	class Conn:
36	    def __init__(self, sender):
37	        self.sender, self.nc = sender, None
38	        self.oid = random.randint(0, 80_000_000)
39	
40	    async def connect(self):
41	        self.nc = await nats.connect(NATS_URL)
42	
43	    def _noid(self):
44	        self.oid += 1
45	        return f"{self.oid:08d}"
46	
47	    async def add(self, feed, side, vol, px, typ="L"):
48	        oid = self._noid()
49	        await self.nc.publish(
50	            f"ex.req.{self.sender}",
51	            f"{self.sender} A {feed} {oid} {side} {int(vol)} {int(px)} {typ}".encode())
52	        return oid
53	
54	    async def cancel(self, feed, oid):
55	        await self.nc.publish(f"ex.req.{self.sender}", f"{self.sender} C {feed} {oid}".encode())
56	
57	    async def close(self):
58	        try:
59	            await self.nc.flush()
60	            await self.nc.drain()
61	        except Exception:
62	            pass
63	
64	
65	class BboCache:
66	    def __init__(self):
67	        self.bbo = {}
68	
69	    async def subscribe(self, nc):
70	        await nc.subscribe("ex.bbo.*", cb=self._cb)
71	
72	    async def _cb(self, msg):
73	        f = msg.data.decode().split()
74	        if len(f) < 6:
75	            return
76	        bid = None if f[2] == "-" else int(f[2])
77	        ask = None if f[4] == "-" else int(f[4])
78	        self.bbo[f[1]] = (bid, int(f[3]), ask, int(f[5]))
79	
80	    def get(self, feed):
81	        return self.bbo.get(feed)
82	
83	
84	def fair_value(b):
85	    if b is None:
86	        return None
87	    bid, bv, ask, av = b
88	    if bid is None and ask is None:
89	        return None
90	    if bid is None:
91	        return float(ask)
92	    if ask is None:
93	        return float(bid)
94	    if ask - bid >= 2 * TICK:          # wide -> mid
95	        return (bid + ask) / 2.0
96	    if bv + av == 0:
97	        return (bid + ask) / 2.0
98	    return (bid * av + ask * bv) / (bv + av)
99	
100	
101	class Book:
102	    """Front-month book reconstruction for the mover (ignores T)."""
103	
104	    def __init__(self):
105	        self.bids, self.asks, self.orders = {}, {}, {}
106	
107	    async def subscribe(self, nc, feed):
108	        await nc.subscribe(f"ex.md.{feed}.*", cb=self._cb)
109	
110	    async def _cb(self, msg):
111	        f = msg.data.decode().split()
112	        t = f[1]
113	        if t == "A":
114	            self.orders[f[2]] = [f[3], int(f[5]), int(f[4])]
115	            lv = self.bids if f[3] == "B" else self.asks
116	            lv[int(f[5])] = lv.get(int(f[5]), 0) + int(f[4])
117	        elif t == "E":
118	            for oid in (f[2], f[3]):
119	                o = self.orders.get(oid)
120	                if o:
121	                    o[2] -= int(f[4])
122	                    lv = self.bids if o[0] == "B" else self.asks
123	                    lv[o[1]] = lv.get(o[1], 0) - int(f[4])
124	                    if lv.get(o[1], 0) <= 0:
125	                        lv.pop(o[1], None)
126	        elif t == "C":
127	            o = self.orders.pop(f[2], None)
128	            if o and o[2] > 0:
129	                lv = self.bids if o[0] == "B" else self.asks
130	                lv[o[1]] = lv.get(o[1], 0) - o[2]
131	                if lv.get(o[1], 0) <= 0:
132	                    lv.pop(o[1], None)
133	
134	    def best_bid(self):
135	        return max(self.bids) if self.bids else None
136	
137	    def best_ask(self):
138	        return min(self.asks) if self.asks else None
139	
140	
141	# --------------------------------------------------------------------------- #
142	async def quoter(sender, contract, deadline):
143	    conn = Conn(sender)
144	    await conn.connect()
145	    bbo = BboCache()
146	    await bbo.subscribe(conn.nc)
147	    rel = CONTRACTS[contract]["rel"]
148	    volume = random.randint(2, 6)
149	    levels = random.randint(4, 8)
150	    offset = random.randint(2, 5)
151	    live, center = [], [None]
152	
153	    async def requote(c):
154	        for _, oid in live:
155	            await conn.cancel(contract, oid)
156	        live.clear()
157	        for i in range(levels):
158	            live.append(("B", await conn.add(contract, "B", volume, c - offset - i)))
159	            live.append(("S", await conn.add(contract, "S", volume, c + offset + i)))
160	        await conn.nc.flush()
161	        center[0] = c
162	
163	    async def on_bbo(_m):
164	        fv = fair_value(bbo.get(FRONT))
165	        if fv is None:
166	            return
167	        c = int(round(fv + rel))
168	        if c != center[0]:
169	            await requote(c)
170	
171	    await requote(START + rel)
172	    await conn.nc.subscribe(f"ex.bbo.{FRONT}", cb=on_bbo)
173	    while now() < deadline:
174	        await asyncio.sleep(0.1)
175	    await conn.close()
176	
177	
178	async def taker(sender, deadline):
179	    # Casual flow: crosses the spread with F (fill-and-kill) orders. F executes
180	    # atomically on the v2.3+ engine (fills in full or rejects), so there is no
181	    # partial-execution handling here.
182	    conn = Conn(sender)
183	    await conn.connect()
184	    bbo = BboCache()
185	    await bbo.subscribe(conn.nc)
186	    while now() < deadline:
187	        await asyncio.sleep(random.uniform(0.05, 0.5))
188	        b = bbo.get(random.choice(ALL))
189	        if b is None:
190	            continue
191	        bid, ask = b[0], b[2]
192	        if random.random() < 0.5 and ask is not None:
193	            await conn.add(random.choice(ALL), "B", 1 + int(20 * random.random() ** 2),
194	                           ask + random.randint(0, 6), "F")
195	        elif bid is not None:
196	            await conn.add(random.choice(ALL), "S", 1 + int(20 * random.random() ** 2),
197	                           bid - random.randint(0, 6), "F")
198	        await conn.nc.flush()
199	
200	
201	async def mover(sender, deadline):
202	    conn = Conn(sender)
203	    await conn.connect()
204	    book = Book()
205	    await book.subscribe(conn.nc, FRONT)
206	    # seed an initial market on every contract
207	    for c in ALL:
208	        init = START + CONTRACTS[c]["rel"]
209	        await conn.add(c, "B", 20, init - 1)
210	        await conn.add(c, "S", 20, init + 1)
211	    await conn.nc.flush()
212	
213	    target = START
214	    next_move = now() + 3
215	    while now() < deadline:
216	        await asyncio.sleep(0.2)
217	        if now() >= next_move:
218	            shock = random.random() < 0.3                 # ~1/3 of moves are sharp
219	            step = random.randint(20, 60) * (2 if shock else 1)
220	            target = max(440, min(760, target + random.choice([-1, 1]) * step))
221	            next_move = now() + random.uniform(2.5, 4.5)
222	        # walk the front toward target: marketable limit at target + market poke
223	        bid, ask = book.best_bid(), book.best_ask()
224	        if bid is None or ask is None:
225	            continue
226	        cur = (bid + ask) / 2.0
227	        if abs(target - cur) < 2:
228	            continue
229	        if target > cur:
230	            need = sum(v for px, v in book.asks.items() if px < target)
231	            await conn.add(FRONT, "B", int(need * 1.2) + 20, target, "L")
232	        else:
233	            need = sum(v for px, v in book.bids.items() if px > target)
234	            await conn.add(FRONT, "S", int(need * 1.2) + 20, target, "L")
235	        await conn.nc.flush()
236	    await conn.close()
237	
238	
239	def main():
240	    random.seed(SEED)
241	    deadline = now() + float(os.environ.get("SIM_DURATION", "86400"))
242	    threads = []
243	
244	    def spawn(fn, *a):
245	        t = threading.Thread(target=lambda: asyncio.run(fn(*a)), daemon=True)
246	        t.start()
247	        threads.append(t)
248	
249	    spawn(mover, "MOVER001", deadline)
250	    time.sleep(1.5)
251	    qn = 0
252	    for c in ALL:
253	        for _ in range(2):
254	            qn += 1
255	            spawn(quoter, f"BGQUOT{qn:02d}", c, deadline)
256	    spawn(taker, "TAKER001", deadline)
257	    spawn(taker, "TAKER002", deadline)
258	    print(f"[market] running on {NATS_URL} (Ctrl-C to stop)", flush=True)
259	    for t in threads:
260	        t.join()
261	
262	
263	if __name__ == "__main__":
264	    main()
265

Now I understand. Reading [sim/market.py:201-236](sim/market.py#L201-L236), the "mover" picks a target **20-60 points away** (doubled for ~1/3 of moves, so up to 120) every 2.5-4.5 seconds, then **sweeps the book** with an order sized at 1.2× everything resting in its path. Meanwhile [sim/market.py:142-175](sim/market.py#L142-L175) shows the background quoters sit 2-5 ticks off the centre.

So with `edge=2` we are permanently at the top of the book, first in line to be run over every three seconds by a 40-point sweep. Rough arithmetic: ~30 sweeps in 95 seconds × 5 lots × ~40 points ≈ 6,000 — which is the loss we measured. The PnL number is trustworthy; the strategy is simply mispriced for this market.

That suggests the edge has to scale with how far the market actually moves. Let me test that directly:
